Her dark hair perfectly pulled back into a high bun, without a single hair out of place. And those heels—the ones she’d confessed to Winn that she wouldn’t mind stepping on her—

“Yes, I’ll see you tomorrow. Bye, babe,” Kara heard from Alex beside her.

Alex set the phone back down on the table, before she cocked her head to the side, catching Kara’s shit-eating grin.

“Bye, babe,” Kara teased, poking at Alex’s side.

Alex swatted Kara’s hand away but failed to hide the blush rising from her cheeks.

“So, speaking of such. Did you ever call that paralegal back?”

Kara sunk back into the cushions of Alex’s couch and blew a stray hair away from her face. Its cousin drifted in front of Kara’s eye, so she resigned to tuck the lot of them behind her ear.

“I sent her a message, but she never got back to me.”

“I’m sorry, Kara.”

Alex tugged one leg up onto the couched and turned to her sister.

“If there’s any way I can make this better, let me know.”
Kara waved her concern away.

“It’s fine. I’m sort of over it by now, anyway.”

Alex picked at the seam on her jeans, unwilling to look her sister in the eyes.

“Is this because of that woman from accounting?” Alex implored.

Kara threw her head back even further into the couch and released a gruff sigh.

“Before you kill Winn, please don’t blame him. He’s so easy to pull information from.”

Kara didn’t respond, hoping Alex would let it be. She did not.

“I knew something was up when he swung by the DEO last week. He’s a terrible liar and even worse at hiding things about my own sister.” Alex rested her palm over Kara’s knee and didn’t pull back until Kara opened her eyes to face her.

“So?” Alex said.

“What?” Kara asked. “What’s there to tell?”
Alex’s brows rose sharply on her forehead.

“Seriously? You catch my conversation with Maggie, and I don’t get to hear a lick about this new woman? How is that fair, Kara?”

“First of all,” Kara began, raising her index finger in front of her face. “I already suspected you two were seeing each other. And second, there really isn’t anything to share. I’ve seen her a couple times at work, but we’ve never spoken, and I know next to nothing about her. She’s just a face, a rather gorgeous face, in the sea of over a thousand people that work in the CatCo building.”

“What’s holding you back from saying hi at least?” Alex asked in a much softer tone this time.

Kara shrugged. The gesture insinuated that she, herself, did not know. But that was a lie. Maybe not a lie to surface Kara, but underneath, in the part of her brain that subconsciously made so many of her decisions, it was a boldfaced lie.

Despite having never met Lena, Kara felt more connected to her than the beautiful woman from accounting (HR?). Her and Lena had spoken, albeit over email, but it counted. They knew things about each other—habits, preferences, and each other’s names for starters. They’d even exchanged gifts that made Kara blush when she thought back to them.

She remembered how foolish she’d felt, walking into WrapZone one evening after work to inquire about the free wraps she’d won. When the boy, barely eighteen, looked back at her with confusion and boredom written across his face, Kara knew she’d misspoken.

“Okay…Well, then who delivered wraps to my office?” she’d asked him.

He did not know, and wanting to spare himself anymore of this conversation, had walked to the back to retrieve his manager.

The manager, an older woman with short, greying hair, reciprocated Kara’s muddled expression at first.

“And you had these delivered to your office?” she’d asked Kara.

Kara shook her head.

“No… a co-worker had them delivered, but I need the receipt to write it off as a work expense.”
Kara wasn’t sure if the manager bought her story, but after another pause, she asked Kara to wait there before going into the back and pulling open a physical log of their delivery orders.

“K. Danvers? At CatCo Worldwide Media?” she repeated after Kara provided her name.

Kara nodded.

“Here,” the lady said. She ripped off a blank page from a notebook sitting beside the front of house phone and scrawled a name and number down. “I don’t have access to past customer receipts, but your co-worker, an L. Luthor, should be able to call into the company line and have that sent over.”

Kara looked down at the ripped half-sheet where the familiar last name and 10-digit WrapZone phone number were written. She folded it and tucked it in her pocket, before thanking the manager and leaving.

Maybe, it was the thrill of the chase that pushed Kara’s thoughts towards Shoebox Lady—Lena. Maybe it was something more.

“Well, I hate to see you all mopey,” Alex said.

She had her elbow digging into the back cushion of the couch and the side of her head resting in her palm.

“Why don’t you come out with me and Maggie this weekend? Maybe meet someone new?”

Kara nodded along but had no plans to join. She’d think of an excuse before the end of the night.

 

The next day, while Kara was going over Cat’s schedule with Siobhan—a duty she would have to take on alone when Kara moved into her new position—an unfamiliar sender appeared in Kara’s inbox.

“One second,” Kara said, turning her laptop away from Siobhan to read it in private.

 

To: Kara Danvers kara.danvers@catcowwm.com

CC:

From: Snapper Carr snapper.carr@catcowwm.com

Subject: First Assignment

 

I need a draft of your first assignment on my desk by EOB Monday. 800-1500 words. Please format it CORRECTLY.

Some lazy reporter in Metropolis wrote some garbage on Tuesday. I need you to learn more. Search Lego bots on the Daily Planet archive and it should be the first result.

 

Snapper

 

 Kara stared blankly at the email. What had she gotten herself into?

Notes for the Chapter:

If you've never read the inspiration for this fic, I highly recommend Attachments as well as Rainbow Rowell's other books.

14. Lena

Summary for the Chapter:

New developments arise with Lena and Kara’s relationship. Unfortunately, it’s never simple when it comes to these two.

Notes for the Chapter:

You have no idea how much I stewed over this chapter. Hint: it was months.

It was well after 5 PM by the time Lena and Shaun were able to leave behind the deceivingly welcoming doors of Catco’s HR department.

“Are you sure you made the right choice?” Shaun asked while they waited for the elevator.

Lena froze, hand hovering in front of her after hitting the chrome down button. The cleaning crew had already been by that floor, because everything was void of handprints and finger smudges.

“I’m kidding,” Shaun added, breaking through the tense silence.

Lena let herself deflate for just a moment.  

“HR should have an offer sent over to our top candidate within the hour. Then it’s just a waiting game from there.”

Lena nodded, not quite as hyped about this eventuality as Shaun was. It was with great relief when the elevator going down arrived and Lena was able to finally part ways with her boss.

“I’ll see you back for our regularly scheduled program on Monday, ‘kay?”

Lena forced a smile and filed into the empty elevator, leaving Shaun standing on the other side, grinning.

 

The 13th floor was a ghost town by the time she arrived back at her temporary office for the last two days. The motion-sensor lights had gone out, leaving the hallway beyond the elevators in a soft glow from the break room vending machines. Lena crossed by the main bullpen as each fluorescent flickered on one at a time.

After scowling down at her half-finished coffee, abandoned when she’d been called up to HR earlier, Lena began to pack up her stuff. She was going to miss the daylight working hours, but realized for the first time, how much she’d come to appreciate the quiet of the night shift.

“Smile, it’s Friday,” came a voice from behind her.

Startled, Lena almost dropped her wireless mouse, before setting it down carefully on the glossy table in front of her. She turned to the side, taking in the four-inch heels, modestly cut dress, and perfectly wavy blonde hair.

“Miss Grant… Hi,” she tapered off, unsure of what to say to the media mogul standing in the doorway.

Miss Grant took a moment to appraise her as she tapped against the metal siding of the doorframe.

“Please tell me that man-child boss of yours is not making you stay any later tonight.”

Lena held back a poorly contained chuckle and shook her head.

“He’s not Miss Grant, no.”

“Then why do you look so glum?” she replied, more to herself than anyone else.

“We decided on a candidate to fill my assistant position.”

Miss Grant crossed her arms in front of her chest and cocked her head to the side.

“Mm, I see. You know, most people would be happy about getting an assistant.”

Lena didn’t meet Miss Grant’s eyes, so she continued.

“I, myself, am on the fence. It can be hard to find a good assistant these days. I suppose I can blame you for having to put myself through that again.”

“Miss Grant, I—”

“But still, assistants can be helpful when you get lucky. I can’t imagine why you wouldn’t want to hire one.”

Just then, the motion-sensor lights in the hall went dark again. Miss Grant took this as her cue to glance down at her watch.

“It’s getting late, I should be going.”

She tapped once more on the doorframe, before turning back to Lena with a mischievous look in her eyes.

“You’re an intelligent woman, Lena. As an actual computer genius—”

“—unsuccessful computer genius,” Lena corrected.

“As I was saying… As an honest-to-God computer genius, I’m surprised you haven’t learned how to solve this assistant problem of yours yet.”

“I-Well, I guess I’m surprised too,” Lena said, quietly.

“Do think on that then,” was Miss Grant’s only response, before she padded down the hall, heels silent against the colour-blocked carpeting.

In a haze, Lena picked up her mug of cold coffee and went to rinse it out in the break room sink. Upon her return, she wasn’t even thinking as she leaned over the desk chair in front of her, and unlocked her computer with the fingerprint scanner dongle hanging off the side. She was not surprised to see a few more red flags had been caught in the past few hours. What she didn’t expect was the unread email notification across the bottom bar of her desktop.

 

To: Lena Luthor

lena.luthor@catcowwm.com

CC:

From: Kara Danvers

kara.danvers@catcowwm.com

Subject: Re: Re: Oloid

 

Good afternoon Lena,

I wish you luck on your quest to find more strange shapes that roll. Maybe you can let me know if you find any of interest or would like to share your own thoughts on wine. I, myself, am impartial, but would rarely turn down a drink.

If you’d like to let me know before Monday, I’ve attached my contact information below.

Your friend,

Kara

 

“Oh, Jesus,” Lena murmured to herself.

She clicked once on the attached contact information and there it was. Kara Danvers. Lena didn’t even need to check the number she still had saved in her contacts to know the one Kara had shared with her did not connect to her desk phone at work. With assured, albeit trembling fingers, Lena edited the K Catco contact and saved the new number to her phone.

 

No matter how many excuses Lena told herself, by the time she forced her body out of bed the following morning, she knew the true culprit behind her insomnia. All night, she’d tossed and turned, eyes flashing over to her phone sitting innocently on her bedside table. By 3 AM, she’d torn back the covers and grabbed the insipid device, stomping into her kitchen, and hiding it inside the breadbox. Maybe if she couldn’t see it, she wouldn’t be tempted.

But no. After only getting a few hours of sleep before finally giving up, Lena resigned herself to the truth that as long as she possessed Kara’s phone number, her mind was a raging battlefield of indecision.

After showering and getting dressed, Lena’s restlessness forced her outside into the late morning sun. She’d been tempted to leave her cell phone at home, but that decision felt akin to tearing off one of her own limbs, so she forewent that option.

She’d just ordered a drink from one of the coffee shops near work—one she rarely got to step inside, thanks to its normal operating hours—when she heard a familiar British accent across the crowded space.

Jack.

He was standing at the back counter behind the winding line to order coffee, with a laptop bag swung over one shoulder and a rolling suitcase standing upright beside him. In his hand was a drink carrier with one coffee cup sitting in the back right spot.

Before Lena could avoid that interaction, their eyes met and he smiled. Perfect.

“Lena, good morning.”

His smile was all teeth as he set the drink carrier down beside him and readjusted the laptop bag’s strap.

“Jack,” she replied, pleasantly. “What are you doing here?”

Even in her most placating tone, the words came out a little too sharp.

“Oh, I…” He smiled again, but it had lost all of the warmth his earlier one had. “I was here on business this week and then we were going to catch a live show tonight, but something came up. Unfortunately, we won’t be able to stick around.”

Lena had been listening to every word he’d said, but there was only really one that stuck out to her. We?

“What show?” she asked instead.

“Oh, this little band called The Bràithrean,” Jack said, as if Lena wouldn’t know them.

As if Lena hadn’t been there with Jack that afternoon back in college when they went to slide their burned disk into the ancient lab CD player, only to find one left behind. They’d eyed each other when they’d seen it, playful expressions dancing across their faces, before Lena closed the lid and pressed play. Through Jack’s endlessly boring titrations, they listened to the musical stylings of the Scottish-Gaelic band. A mix of rock and folk music Lena was unfamiliar with. It had been a joke at first to listen to the whole album, but by the end, they were reluctant to try and track down the CD’s owner.

Six months later, they’d purchased all three of the band’s albums and were driving back and forth across the country to attend as many of the band’s North American tour shows as possible on their severely underpaid grad-student wages.

It was a summer of three to ten hour drives, of scorching August heat that left the dashboard of Jack’s car hot enough to burn the soles of Lena’s feet. Switching shifts on the overnight drive back so they wouldn’t have to stop and pay for an overpriced hotel room that smelled of cigarettes and musty linens.

Despite the hours they’d spend listening to the band’s albums on repeat on the way there and the hour-long live set at the venue, Lena still hooked her phone up to the AUX output and listened to her favourites when it was her turn to drive. Jack was a light sleeper in those days so she’d had to turn the volume down on his after-market radio to LOW and hum quietly to herself. There was never another time when she felt truly suspended within the music. Jack’s little silver Hyundai was her bubble and the hum of the engine and occasional passing truck on the highway, her only tether to what lay beyond the lyrics.

She’d never connected with the lyrics the way she could with other songs, but it was the escapism the songs gave her that she truly relished. For a moment, she could envision herself as the lad in their songs, complaining of hardship and lost lovers in a pub at one o’clock in the morning. There were so many emotions and feelings in the songs—something Lena felt she lacked on too many occasions. For Jack to take that away from her felt worse than the nights he’d come home, barely giving her a second look before getting ready for bed.

“Like I said, we were going to catch their show tonight, but something came up,” he said with a shrug.

“So you’re flying back to Metropolis soon?” Lena asked, stalling as she prayed her drink order would be called soon.

“Yes. I believe I am meeting with some directors from Biospheer tonight,” he said, unconvinced.

“Biospheer?” Lena muttered under her breath.

That was a name she hadn’t heard in a long while. Back when they’d been working together, two naïve college grads striving to cure cancer and the like, Biospheer was the name he’d registered their company under. He’d been pretty chuffed at the clever name. Lena thought it was rather presumptuous to go about naming a company that had made no real advancements in anything yet.

Three years later, it was also the company they’d agreed to sell when Jack had been offered an engineering position at a highly esteemed biotech company in Metropolis and Lena had all but given up on her AI program. The investor had spent years in the business and was excited by what their Nano-cells could do. Even though it wasn’t the advancement Lena wanted to give the public, it was better than nothing.

Before Lena could ask any clarifying questions on the subject, a woman with dark hair had sidled up beside Jack and placed her own coffee in the drink carrier.

“You ready?” the woman asked as she stepped further into his space and placed a territorial hand across his chest.

Jack smiled down at the woman, while she barely gave Lena a cursory glance. After shifting awkwardly on the spot, Jack picked up their drinks in one hand and grabbed the handle of his suitcase in the other.

“Of course,” he replied.

Lena tried to catch his eyes before they left, but it was as if he’d forgotten she was even there.

 

Lena was insane. She was being impractical. It was just a number and yet, she’d almost forgotten to make herself dinner that night. She’d been staring down at her half-finished plate of stir fry when she decided that a bored Lena was a potentially reckless Lena. That was how she’d ended up at a bar in downtown National City, just past 8:30 at night. The Bràithrean were supposedly performing at 9:00, or so their very unhelpful Facebook page had announced via a grainy Instagram post last week. Lena needed a distraction and what better distraction was there than Scottish-Gaelic rock music?

The bar they were performing at was located in the historical district, nestled between an old movie theatre, that was apparently only showing the Rocky Horror Picture Show that week, and an Asian Fusion restaurant. Lena wasn’t old by any means, but she still felt overdressed and out of place inside the cozy space. Her slacks were a little too wrinkle-free and the low-cut blouse she’d chosen on a whim was more fitting for a boardroom than a grungy bar.  Still, Lena ordered a beer, not trusting their whiskey selection, and found a seat to the left of the stage (if you could call a slightly raised platform squished between tables a stage).

Ten minutes until the band was set to perform, Lena looked up from her phone and noticed just how busy the bar had gotten. The round, four-to-five seater tables in front of the stage were packed and the the high-tops like the one Lena was perched at were all taken.

“Excuse me ma’am,” a man dragging a cord said from beside her.

He passed it behind Lena and snaked it across the stage in front of her.

“Sorry,” she replied, too delayed for him to even hear.

Lena pressed a nail into a crack running down the centre of the table and bit at the side of her lip. This place was not her scene. It was too loud, too lively, too—Kara?

Lena tried not to let her eyes drag across the room as she watched three women make their way through the front doors of the bar and up the stairs to the 2nd story loft. The two that led the way were not familiar to Lena. Both had darker hair, one brown and wavy, the other auburn and clipped shorter at the shoulders, and had their hands clasped as they pushed their way through the crowd. It was the third woman trailing behind them that had Lena gripping the side of her pint glass a little too hard.

She was wearing a snug pair of jeans and a burgundy sweater with the sleeves rolled up to her elbows. Her hair was up in a high ponytail and a pair of glasses sat low on her nose. It was not at all like the superhero’s usual red and blue getup, but there was something about the woman that overwhelmingly screamed Kara. Lena didn’t know how or why, but this woman was her.

A bang from the stage crew testing out the bass drum jerked Lena’s eyes away. She glanced around herself at the people nearby, but no one had seemed to notice her trance-like fixation. When she looked back up to the second floor, searching for those unmistakeable blue eyes again, she was lost to the masses.

At a quarter past nine, Lena’s beer was gone and the band still hadn’t taken the stage yet. Lena shrugged off her light jacket and left it to hang over the back of her chair. The bar was packed this time. There were droves of college kids waiting to pounce on any bit of unoccupied real estate. Lena tried to call over the din to order just one more beer, when another body moved into her space. Lena stiffened, dreading the feel and stench of someone else’s sweat covered arms brushing against hers, when a distinctly feminine voice quelled that anxiety.

“You wanted another House blonde, right?” the woman asked as she placed a gentle hand on the back of Lena’s elbow.

Lena’s forthcoming answer died on her lips as she turned to see a very different kind of blonde in her peripheral vision. Lena must have communicated her approval in some nonverbal way, as the woman then stepped forward and easily parted the crowd of people. She garnered the bartender’s attention promptly and was sliding over a freshly poured pint of beer before Lena could fully comprehend what had happened.

“Oh, thank you,” Lena said, when she’d eventually regained her ability to speak.

Their gazes met; Lena’s was searching, trying to recognize National City’s hero behind a pair of black rimmed glasses. The woman shifted, uncomfortable, and slid the frames higher up her nose as a light pink tinged her cheeks.

From behind them, someone knocked into Lena, shoving her forward as some of the head on her beer tipped over the edge of the glass, coating Lena’s fingers in a sticky foam. She switched hands, cursing as she reached for a napkin to dry her hand off. When she looked back up, the woman was glaring behind Lena, her chest seeming to stutter on her next inhale.

“Kara?”

Both of them turned to see the auburn woman approaching the bar with the same determination.

“Hey,” she continued, slightly out of breath when she reached them.

She spent a few seconds longer than Lena felt comfortable with, sizing her up, before turning her attention to… Kara?

“Hey, Alex,” Kara replied.

Alex. Alex Danvers, Kara’s sister—Supergirl’s sister. So she’d been spot on. She knew she was. She didn’t miss things like that. But that probably was not the news Lena needed right now.

“Maggie found us a table in the front room,” Alex said, facing Kara now.

“The front?” Kara replied.

“Yeah. Is that not okay?”

Kara’s eyes flickered toward Lena, unsure, before she shook her head firmly.

“Okay. Good. I’ll uh, meet you over there?”

It sounded like a question, but Kara didn’t respond, so she just ducked away.

“So, how much do I owe you?” Lena finally asked, voice sounding strained.

It felt like some invisible force had a vice-like grip around her throat, making even the simplest of statements impossible to force out.

“Oh, don’t worry about it.”

“Well, thank you. Again.”

If the bar hadn’t been so loud, Lena was sure Kara would have heard the pounding of her heart against the inside of her rib cage. Hell, she was Supergirl. Maybe she still could.

Just then, a piercing sound cut through the noise around them. Lena cringed, covering one ear with her free hand. A man on stage unplugged the microphone, stopping the high-pitched feedback.

“Sorry about that folks,” he said once the audio system had been reconnected. “We’re about to get started in a moment.”

Lena recognized the accent, but felt conflicted as to whether or not the heavy feeling it caused in her chest was a good or bad thing. She decided to reflect on that later, as she turned to find Kara again. But Kara was gone and across the room, two men were circling Lena’s table. With a sigh, Lena took her beer back to her seat.

 

The band was definitely older. Grey lines peppered the hair around the lead singer’s ears and aging lines appeared at the creases of their eyes every time they yelled into the mics. Lena also noticed The Bràithrean had replaced their drummer since the last time she’d seen them live. But other than some years and a new face, everything was just as she remembered it.

They started their set with a hit off of their second album. Even bar patrons who were unfamiliar with the band recognized the intro riff, sitting taller in their seats when the familiar bass line started. A few even sang along to the nonsensical humming.

As a young twenty-something, Lena could admit that she’d sang along to the words she knew after having a few shots in her. But after barely starting in on her second beer, her face was now the poster-child for indifference. She leaned forward in her chair, letting the familiar music drown out every anxious worry that threatened to upend the meagre contents of her stomach.

After moving through some of their heavier rock hits, the lead singer moved away from centre stage and stepped toward Lena’s side. He picked a cello up that had been lying down on its side. It was an acoustic, but a pick-up had been installed near the bridge to capture the sound better. A minute later, he was dragging the bow across the lowest string, sending a skin-tingling sensation through Lena’s body. When the electric guitar and drum beat came in eight counts later, Lena was transfixed.

Their slower songs were never Jack’s favourites. In certain moods, Lena preferred them. There was something overwhelming in the way the song slowly crescendoed as it approached the final chorus. As if not even noticing that by the time they reached those repeated lyrics one final time, all of the instruments had made their way in, filling the room with five distinct, yet well-incorporated sounds.

“A friend of yours?”A voice said from behind her.

Lena looked over her shoulder to see Kara staring at her with a raised brow.

“I-no. I was just admiring the cello. You don’t see many of them in bands. Fiddles, sure. But not—”

Kara’s neutral expression broke into a smile.

“—Oh, you’re just messing with me.”

“Maybe.” Kara smirked, before nodding to the empty seat across from Lena.

“Please,” Lena said, inviting her to sit.

“So… Not cellos?” Kara asked, as the band set up for their next song.

“Not cellos,” Lena confirmed.

As the band started in on a song Lena had never heard before, she noticed Kara’s bare forearms resting on the table between them. Both chairs had been turned to face the stage, so they sat closer together than usual. Lena couldn’t believe these arms, covered in light blonde hair and the occasional freckle, were the same ones that had saved a school bus just a week ago on the news.

Lena wondered if she ever got cold or if she was always as warm and radiant as she appeared. Maybe if Lena moved her own hand, lightly tapping her half-finished glass of beer, closer to Kara’s, she could feel—

“Do you play?”

“What?” Lena turned in her seat.

“Do you play?” Kara motioned to the cellist currently counting rests with his foot softly tapping the stage.

“Oh. No,” Lena replied, shifting both hands to her lap. “I used to, but that was a long time ago.”

She turned her attention back to the band, but Kara wasn’t finished. She leaned fractionally closer to Lena, shifting in her seat so her voice didn’t have to go above a whisper.

“I bet you were pretty good.”

“Excuse me?” Lena blanched when she realized how loud she’d been, but steeled her face as quickly as she could.  

“At the cello,” Kara said. “Were you not?” she added, challengingly.

Lena bit her lip, considering.

“No, you’re right. I was very good,” she answered, boldly.

Kara tried to hide a smile behind her hand as she leaned on her elbow, no longer even trying to face the band playing on stage.

A moment passed as the lead-guitar finished the outro-solo, fading into silence. Then—

“Do you think it’s wrong to feel a strong sense of nostalgia for something that was awful at the time?” Lena asked, surprising herself even.

Kara didn’t blink, attention never leaving Lena. She studied her this time, searching gaze making Lena feel as if all of the secrets she held beneath her mask of quiet dignity were suddenly surfacing like a body from beneath opaque water.  

“I think circumstance can play such a large role, that it drowns out all the good.”

Lena nodded, noticing how far away Kara seemed as the next song started.

 

Even when the band finished their set, Kara made no move to stand up from the chair across from Lena. She bought Lena another beer when the condensation from her nearly empty glass had run down the table to where their elbows were now brushing up against each other.

A more sober Lena had been right. Kara ran very warm. So warm she figured the jacket she’d brought would be unneeded if she could only lean into Kara’s side and never leave.

Beneath the cacophony of clinking glasses, screeching chair legs, and hollers from patrons quickly leaving tipsy behind, they spoke in hushed tones as if every word out of their mouths was a closely kept secret only meant for each other. They talked about superficial things—the best place to buy El Salvadorian pupusas in National City; the coolest place they’d ever visited (Nara, Japan in Lena’s opinion); and where they’d move to if money and personal responsibilities weren’t an issue (Never staying in one spot for too long, for Kara).

To Lena’s surprise, though, it was her and not Kara that didn’t face trepidation when breaching the harder topics. Maybe it was the alcohol. Maybe it was knowing that the beautiful woman in front of her was an alien from another universe. When Kara followed up on their earlier conversation about Lena’s cello playing as a child, she didn’t sugarcoat the way her mother would watch over every private lesson, providing her own criticism, even when her teacher had nothing negative to say. When Kara had more questions about her summer following the band, her summer with Jack, her years with Jack, she didn’t feel judged. Even when it was obvious that she’d let Jack go. Even when it was obvious that Lena was not where she wanted to be, in life, in so many things.

But there was always something slightly detached about Kara. It made sense, of course, that Kara wouldn’t immediately open up to Lena about her superhero alter ego. That wasn’t what made Lena hesitate every time Kara would look away or down at her black phone screen. There was something stilted in Kara’s smile. Like she wasn’t letting it broaden as wide as Lena knew it could. But maybe Lena was wrong. It was one thing to watch Kara on TV and hear her voice through her messages on Lena’s computer screen at Catco, but a whole different thing to actually know the complex woman in front of her.

 

By the time Lena finished her third beer, the bar had grown considerably quieter. Lena was reluctant to check her phone, instead enjoying her time with Kara as if it existed outside of their normal timelines—two people who were not supposed to meet, but had.

“I think you should definitely start your own line of athletic apparel,” Kara said laughing.

Lena snorted as she doubled over, hands braced against the side of the table. They were discussing the pros and cons of yoga pants—Lena paying close attention when Kara had included how great it made her ass look, in her pros list.

“I will do no such thing,” she replied, feeling her cheeks warming under Kara’s insistently eager grin.

“No, no,” Kara replied. She reached forward and placed a hand on Lena’s arm so she’d lean back and face Kara.  “I think you’re really onto something combining yoga pants and suspenders.” She couldn’t even keep a straight face as she said this, forcing Lena to hide her own reddened cheeks in her hands.

“Please, no,” Lena eventually replied, resolutely.

She turned to see that Kara’s attention had shifted away from her gaze. Kara’s hand was suspended between them, before her fingers tucked a loose strand of hair behind Lena’s ear. Lena couldn’t help the way she leaned into the touch, body swaying in her seat.

“What would we call my clothing line?” Lena asked a second later, trying not to think about the way Kara’s eyes had flitted down from Lena’s for a moment.

“Well…” Kara trailed off, swallowing.

Lena could see the muscles in her forearms straining beneath her skin as she leaned against the table.

“We could come up with some clever name, maybe play off the suspenders.”

Lena coughed back an obnoxious laugh.

“Or we could go with something classy and simple. Your name maybe, kind of like Chanel or Louis Vuitton.”

“I agree. Suspenders are a very classy thing, after all,” Lena murmured, watching as Kara’s knee bumped into her own beneath the table.

“So it’s agreed, then. We’ll name your new athletic wear line…”

Lena drew in a breath. “After me,” she finished, lamely.

Kara’s face didn’t fall at Lena’s reluctance to introduce herself, but there was something a bit off, as if she’d lost focus.

As Lena argued internally with her tipsy-brain on whether telling Kara who she was, was possibly the worst idea she’d ever had, the woman across from her was suddenly no longer sitting in the adjacent chair.

“I’ve got to go,” Kara said, quickly rounding the table.

“What?” Lena straightened in her chair.

“I’ve got to drive Alex, my sister, home,” she said rushed.

Knowing her frantic movements were most likely Supergirl related, Lena spared Kara by not mentioning that she’d noticed Alex and the other woman leaving a good thirty minutes earlier. Instead she just nodded, a solemn look of understanding on her face. Maybe this emergency’s terrible timing was actually for the better.

 

Notes for the Chapter:

I decided on the end to this story, finally, and have thus, included a tentative chapter count.

Also, I have been working on a new supercorp fake relationship AU this past month (which can be very distracting when it comes to finishing Attachments). I don’t want the updates to drag on forever with it, so I will not start posting until the entire first draft is complete.

15. Lena

Summary for the Chapter:

Everything begins to fall apart at once.

Notes for the Chapter:

I feel like i should add the Tag: Lena Luthor needs a hug, to this fanfiction.

It was wrong. Lena knew it was wrong. Yet, she’d let Kara sit beside her the entire night, indulging in the same easy-going conversations she’d read many times before between Kara and her friends. Still, Lena hated lying, but what other option did she have? Maybe Kara did not suspect that Lena, Shoebox Lady Lena, was the one reading all of their private messages right now. Maybe nothing disastrous would happen if she finally introduced herself to Kara. But what if it did?

Lena could handle bad news when it hit suddenly: learning that Lillian was perfectly fine when Lena cut all ties with the Luthor family; learning that her AI program designed to target cancer cells and stimulate healthy tissue growth was once again, a failure; and even learning from Jack a few years back, that his cancer had returned. What Lena could not handle—would not handle—was the anticipation of something bad yet to happen.

Just then, her phone chimed from inside her jacket pocket, alerting her that her ride was two minutes away. Lena pulled the zipper up on her jacket and stepped out from the bar’s awning. The light rain falling in front of her made her skin itch, so she hunched further forward, searching the street for any cars that looked like they might be pulling up.

A moment later, a silver car with the ride-share company’s logo stuck to the inside of the windshield came to a stop and turned on its hazards. The passenger’s side window rolled down and an older woman in her late fifties, maybe sixties, called out to her.

“Excuse me, hon. Are you Lena?” She held her own phone out, arm extended as far as it could across the empty passenger’s seat in an attempt to show Lena the screen.

It was way too far away to read given the darkness and the way the rain partially obscured her vision. Lena nodded anyway and stepped forward into the road.

“Yes, that’s me,” she said, tentatively.

The woman smiled brightly at Lena, before the door locks clicked, and she motioned for Lena to get in.

Maybe it was the way Lena’s lips were pulled tight or her clear avoidance to make eye-contact with her driver that halted any further conversation once the car merged back into traffic. As the historic district slowly transformed into quiet downtown apartments and businesses closed up for the day, Lena sat still in her seat, trying to ignore the foreign smells of the stranger’s car.

There was a radio show on; two women’s voices—one with a southern drawl and the other too hard to place—floated out from the car’s speakers.

“We often hold ourselves to higher standards than anyone else around us,” the southern woman was saying.

The other agreed with her, just as Lena’s driver hummed in her own acknowledgement.

“Can you think of a time in your own life when you expected too much from yourself?” the woman continued.

Instead of the other radio host answering, the car was quiet. Lena looked away from the window to the rearview mirror where her driver was whispering something to herself. After another few seconds of silence, the radio host continued talking as if Lena had answered the question herself.

Lena wished that this was a time when she was expecting too much from herself. The truth was though, that despite it being her job at Catco to filter through its employees’ messages, it wasn’t Lena’s job to pry into Supergirl or her friends’ lives. When she thought about telling Kara what she’d been up to these past couple months—when she thought about Kara’s reaction—it made her feel sick. Like truly nauseous to the point that she was tempted to ask her driver to pull the vehicle over at any moment.

Lena drew a slow breath in through her nose and exhaled, pressing her palms flat against her stomach. This time, when Lena looked back out the window, it wasn’t the reflections of other tinted car windows that she saw, but a memory from high school. Fourteen-year-old Lena sitting in the back seat of Landon’s older sister’s red Jetta. The stack of old PC Gamer magazines in the back pocket of the seat instead of the small water bottles her ride-share driver had set out. The floor, far from clean and always accumulating bits of trash between the winter mats. It always smelled like a thrift store. Sometimes, there’d be sweaters or coats left back there for weeks. When Lena and Landon were both in the back, they’d have to push the small pile of clothes to the centre seat, forming a wall between them like they were children again, sharing a bed, but afraid to get cooties during sleepovers.

Landon’s older sister Beth loved self-help audiobooks and inspirational memoirs (though Lena wasn’t sure if she did so ironically or not). They were always narrated by women with soothing voices that seemed a bit too sure of themselves, droning on about seeing value within yourself. Between each spiel about self-worth, there’d be these open, unanswered questions, followed by long pauses in the recordings. Lena thought the whole thing was bullshit.

On most trips to the cinema or back to Landon’s house from school, Beth’s best friend would turn to her in the front passenger’s seat during the moments of silence and crack a joke—usually an inside joke that referenced something Lena did not understand at the time. Beth would always erupt in this warm and breathy laugh, meeting her friend’s gaze across the console and sometimes nudging her friend’s shoulder gently, before returning her hand to the stick-shift.

In the backseat, while Landon flipped through the pages of his magazines, Lena would stiffen, something odd and unsettling stirring in her chest, before she’d realize the reaction was unfounded. Then the audiobook’s narrator would resume, and Beth’s friend would turn away.

On rare occasions, it would just be the three of them: Beth, Landon, and Lena. Her and Landon would still have to put up with Beth’s audiobooks, but it was nice having a driver who didn’t pester them about school or shoot them suggestive brow raises, implying there was more to their relationship than friendship.

Lena always took the backseat on these days and would listen to the short conversations between siblings during the pauses in the audiobook recordings. Sometimes the conversations were stilted, as would be expected given their four-year age gap. Other times, Beth would tease Landon about something frivolous and Lena would laugh, because it was always so Landon. Beth would grin, catching Lena’s eyes in the rearview mirror, and Lena’s chest would stir again with a different odd feeling. It was always heavy, but never unpleasant.

That was the way Lena felt when she thought of Kara: heavy, but not unpleasant. Maybe even more than pleasant if she was honest with herself.

As Lena lied in bed that night, body tucked under the covers up to her shoulders, she stared down at K Catco’s contact information. No matter how ridiculous she felt avoiding the little message icon, she just couldn’t do it.

“I’m sorry,” Lena murmured into her pillow, before locking her phone and turning over.

 

Though thoughts of Kara occupied most of the good real estate in Lena’s mind the following morning, she couldn’t seem to shake her run in with Jack at the café earlier. Despite being a direct witness to his erratic behaviour recently, there was an overwhelming piece of her that sincerely missed his company. When faced with a tricky problem, he was the best sounding board, like a reflection that didn’t hide the parts she chose to ignore.

Every time she had ran into an issue with her AI program, he’d pull her out of the hole she’d dug herself into and take her back to the basics.

 

“The nano cells aren’t recognizing the cancer anymore,” she’d confessed one night over dinner.

Jack had looked up from his carton of General Tso chicken, sauce dripping over the edge and getting precariously close to his dark, calloused fingers.

“Well, brutally stabbing those poor dumplings isn’t going to change that,” he joked, before settling into a neutral expression. “Listen, Lena…” He reached out and steadied her other hand, restlessly spinning the class ring around her index finger. “What’s different now?”

“What?” she asked, leaning back from her container, chopsticks abandoned with a soft clunk on the coffee table.

“What’s different now from when they were targeting the cells proficiently?”

She thought back to M601, the most recent line of mice subjected to radiation that had been doing well for the past 12 weeks, only to suddenly light up like a Christmas tree when she’d done a CT scan of their lungs yesterday.

“Nothing has changed,” she replied, frustrated. “All other variables have remained constant. It just stopped working.”

She pushed off the couch and paced toward the front window, watching their neighbour across the street hunched over his flowerbed, aggressively yanking weeds from the ground.

“Well—” His voice was quiet, still seated on the other side of the room. “It’s got to be another variable then.”

She wanted to lash out at him, but he was right. She knew he was right and she hated that just as much as she was thankful for it.

“What aren’t you monitoring?” he asked a minute later after her lack of a response.

The dim hum of her neighbour’s trimmer came to life as Lena turned back to meet Jack’s eyes. He stared back at her, his serious countenance shifting to a frown.

“What?” he asked, brows furrowed.

“I haven’t been monitoring the cancer cells,” she said, a wicked grin breaking across her face.

Her feet were hitting the stairs, already headed for her second floor office, before he had a chance to reply.

“Lena!” he called. “Your dinner.”

She ignored his protest, but still spun around halfway up the stairs.

“I haven’t been monitoring the cancer cells,” she exclaimed again, sticking her face back around the corner; her body hung over the first few stairs as she braced her weight on the outdated, oak railing. “The AI has been successfully targeting them before I’ve had a chance to record anything.”

Jack had laughed at that, full bellied and filling the space of their small living room.

“I don’t think I’ve ever heard anyone sound so excited about missing data before.”

“Well, you don’t get out much, so I’m not surprised,” she’d jabbed back, a smirk tempting the corner of her lips.

 

Just then, Miss Grant’s words from the other night came back to her.

“You’re an intelligent woman, Lena.”

“I’m surprised you haven’t learned how to solve this assistant problem.”

“That—that sneaky woman,” Lena muttered out loud as she made her way to the front hall of her apartment.

She threw open the door to her powder room, or more accurately, her junk closet, because what was the point of a half-bath if no one ever visited? She still had a few unpacked boxes from her move—belongings she’d had no use for as of late but didn’t have the mental fortitude to part with.

Buried under old makeup and a bag of miscellaneous trinkets that had once sat atop her desk at her last job, was her old laptop. The top was still stained from that time she’d spilled a brand-new bottle of Poloxamer 188 in the lab and hadn’t cleaned it off thoroughly.

It took a painstakingly long fifteen minutes to finally come back to life. Lena was seriously doubting if the little LED light on the side would ever flicker on. While she waited for about a billion Windows updates, she walked laps around her tiny kitchen island, sipping at her first mug of coffee every time she stopped to check on the loading bar.

Eventually, after a second mug had been filled and she was sitting comfortably at one of the bar chairs behind her counter, the login screen filled her vision. She guessed her old password on the second try and then there it was: at least forty shortcuts, including the old Visual Studio icon in the bottom left corner. All of them were set against a background photo of the sun setting behind a barren stretch of I-15. A pixelated outline of Jack hunched over the hood of his Hyundai sat in the foreground. Lena bit her lip as a swell of pressure filled her chest. She had once done a deep dive on visual imagery and the rule of thirds. This photo was not a great example of that, but Jack had admired it every time he’d pass behind her while they worked.

Pushing the aching nostalgia from her mind, Lena pulled a few AI references from her old work, then set about her new project. It was child’s play compared to what she used to work on but coding a successful AI program was definitely not like riding a bicycle (unless that bicycle was a perpetually shifting amorphous contraption).

She worked through lunch, only stopping to root through her fridge for a second before giving up and settling for a third cup of coffee. By the time the sun was low in the sky and creating an irritating glare across her computer screen, Lena had a fairly comprehensive program before her. It wasn’t fancy, by any means or user-friendly. Thankfully, Lena was planning on being the only user, so that wasn’t really an issue. All that was left was a test-run and for that, she’d have to wait until work the following night.

With a sigh, Lena locked her computer, then swivelled in her bar chair. When she hopped down, her legs felt like jello from hanging down for so long and her butt was numb where it had pressed into the unforgiving plastic backing of the IKEA chair.

She spotted her phone, still sitting beside her laptop on the counter and was tempted to open it up. It had been easy to avoid thinking about Kara while she’d been coding, but now, with the next few hours free, she could feel her hand itching to reach out. She took a deep breath, before sliding her fingers around the device, and picking it up. She stalked into the bedroom and closed the curtains, before sitting on the edge of her bed. In the dark, it was less nerve-wracking to unlock the phone and navigate over to her messages. Kara’s contact was the second suggestion down, even after she’d only typed a K into the To: line on the New Message screen.

Her finger hovered over it, before she let out a heaving breath.

“No,” Lena scolded herself.

Not yet.

She flung the phone across the duvet, watching as the still open screen eventually faded to black.

“Not yet.”

 

Instead of seeing her new assistant on the other side of the elevator doors the following night, it was just Shaun.

“You busy?” Shaun asked the moment she stepped off.

Lena adjusted her oversized purse—the one holding her old laptop—on her shoulder and gave him a quizzical look.

“I’m just joshing you,” he said, waving his hand through open air as if he were nudging her shoulder from ten feet away.

He pulled the doors to the IT floor open and looked back over his shoulder, motioning for her to follow, as if she had any other options at this point.

“So, the new hire is in orientation this week, but I asked HR if she could start late on Friday so you can at least meet her and set her up with the basics.”

There was a basket with some cords sticking out, waiting for them in the break area. Shaun pulled a small laptop from underneath a wireless keyboard and mouse and set it down with a thunk on the stained round table.

“This should be ready to go for her, but if you have any issues, just let IT know.” He looked up at Lena with a cheeky grin, before plowing on, explaining where she could sit on Friday and how to configure the desk phone, so she’d have her own employee number.

By the time he left, Lena was so anxious, her hands were shaking; her first attempt to login to her work computer with the finger scanner even failed, because she couldn’t keep her index finger still enough. Once she got both laptops ready to go though, running the AI program on her company issued laptop was seamless.

It started scanning employee messages, emails, and attachments from the beginning of last year, looking for key words that might giveaway Supergirl’s secret, then promptly removing and overwriting them on the server. It was designed to learn from the Superfriends’ messages, catching more than just the odd red flag.

Lena took a working lunch late that evening, pulling a ready-to-go chicken salad sandwich from her bag just shy of midnight as she monitored the program’s progress. By 2 a.m., it had filtered through a month of data, already at an 87% accuracy rate. With a little more time, Lena was sure she could raise that number to 99.

She held herself a little taller that morning as she walked home. It had rained again at some point during her shift, so the air was heavy and clung to her skin. She didn’t seem to mind though, even taking the long route around the park instead of cutting through it like she usually did to get home sooner.

It was with this unyielding sense of accomplishment that she pulled her phone from the back pocket of her slacks and fired off a message to Kara.

[Lena] Hey Kara, it’s Lena.

She stopped in the middle of the sidewalk and stared down at the screen. Wow. That was it then, wasn’t it? She’d finally texted Kara on nothing more than a whim and the weightless sensation of finally ridding an anxiety.

If she no longer had to scan through Kara and her friends’ messages, maybe she could move forward with an actual relationship with the woman. That was a novel concept: a genuine friendship.

For the first time since she’d crossed paths with Kara through the impersonal screen of her Catco computer, Lena allowed herself to scroll through her social media in bed, smiling at endless photos of Kara and her friends. The last thing she remembered before her brain tricked her into closing her eyes were Kara’s own—bright blue and just as impressionable as the first time Lena saw her across the park in her Supergirl regalia, all those weeks ago.

Lena woke later that day to the warm orange glow of sunlight peeking around the edges of her curtains. She rolled over, only to feel the outline of something hard underneath her. She lifted her back, still warm from lying under her bed covers, and felt around until she retrieved her phone.

Huh.

She must have fallen asleep before plugging it in to charge that morning. The battery was thankfully not completely drained.

Before twisting back to face her side table, she glanced at the notifications on the Lock Screen. There were the usual suspects: a notification from her credit card that a payment was due in seven days and a reoccurring reminder to water her plants that had died months ago back in Metropolis. What had her nearly rolling off the bed was the third item down. A text message. A text message from K Danvers.

Just as quickly as the spike of excitement had hit her square in the chest, a different sort of weight settled there when she realized it wasn’t just one message. It was many.

Lena opened her phone, scrolling up to the top of their conversation, back to the very beginning. Lena knew something was very wrong, even before scanning down to the other messages. No one replied to a simple greeting with paragraphs of text if everything was just peachy.

[K Catco] It’s you, isn’t it?

[K Catco] You’ve been the one erasing all of the messages about…

[K Catco] You know…

[K Catco] Winn was suspicious, but I didn’t think about it too hard, because I figured if that were the case you would have said something, right? Who just let’s someone continuously do stupid things like that?

[K Catco] Never mind. Sorry.

[K Catco] It’s just…

[K Catco] I don’t know Lena.

[K Catco] I don’t like to swear but Rao Lena!

[K Catco] Do you know how Winn figured it out?

[K Catco] Heck, you probably do, considering how much you buried our sensitive information. Winn said the data wasn’t just erased, but the storage space where it should have been had been overwritten with corrupted, irretrievable files. He was ready to escalate the issue to the DEO.

[K Catco] Wait, forget I said that.

K Catco] Although what’s the point now.

[K Catco] You probably already know who that is anyway.

Lena sucked in a breath, but continued to read on, unable to tear herself away like she was watching the aftermath of a gruesome accident.

[K Catco] I stuck up for you by the way. I convinced him not to say anything.

[K Catco] So like, I’m not going to rat you out or anything.

[K Catco] Although I guess I should be asking the same from you seeing as you know now.

[K Catco] I just don’t know Lena.

There was nothing after that. A final end to the black bubbles of text.

Lena quickly tapped over to reply, but her fingers stalled. She typed out and erased at least five different messages to Kara before collapsing back into bed. What could she say? I fucked up?

Maybe… No. Lena set the phone down on her bed and looked up at her ceiling. Maybe she shouldn’t tell Kara that, but it was true. It was her worst nightmare–worse than being forced to relive every argument with Jack, every horribly pleasant conversation with her mother, every pitying question from her former coworkers the day the gossip vine had finally caught up with her.

She did the only thing she thought was right, the only thing Kara deserved (of which Lena could offer her now).

[Lena] I’m sorry, Kara. I promise your secret will always be safe with me.

 

Somehow, despite the catastrophic collapse to the only thing Lena had found true purpose in since moving to National City, time persisted.

At four p.m. she made a large wok of vegetable stir fry for her lunches the next couple nights. At six, she pulled out the box she’d found her old laptop in and tossed out most of the other junk sitting at the bottom. At six-thirty, she rummaged through the garbage bag now sitting slumped against the wall in her front hall and pulled out a few of the trinkets she’d thrown out. It was sentimental and illogical, but the stupid wine-cork Christmas tree a former coworker had made for the office reminded Lena of a message exchange between Kara and Winn last December; and of course, her debugging rubber duck Jack had bought her when she’d graduated university was dressed in a Superman suit, albeit a very faded and partially worn away one. When seven-thirty rolled around, Lena packed her lunch and a new batch of DVDs into her bag and headed out the door to work.

And through it all, the worst part about it, was that nothing felt amiss. Everything was exactly the same. Well, almost… If there was anything she could take solace in, it was the absence of any Supergirl-related red flags from that Tuesday onward. Still, as she sat in Dilworth’s squeaky chair at work, watching her program become increasingly accurate with its detection of Super-related messages, she couldn’t help but feel the immense loss of no longer being a covert part of team Kara.

It felt so wrong to verify the flags, but it was still her job (for now). Lena made it all the way to her Thursday lunch break, periodically checking on her code, while binging 80s movies on the small TV in Dilworth’s office. It was just past one a.m. when she felt her stomach protest loudly over the crackling sound of Watts sliding up onto the top of an oil drum to face Keith in his greasy mechanic jumpsuit.

She hit pause on the DVD player and turned in her chair to glance briefly over at her computer. There was an new email in her inbox for once. She opened it, skimming the words from one of Shaun’s bosses. She needed to start on some software update once her assistant was all set up. Lena didn’t have the heart to open the attachments, though. She might be caught off guard next week if she didn’t at least peruse it a bit, but then again, she’d probably be thankful if they let her go (as long as it came with a nice severance package that is).

 

She returned to her desk after stopping by Wrap Zone and was fully prepared to jump back into her favourite 80s classic, Some Kind of Wonderful, when she realized she’d never actually reviewed the Superfriends messages from Monday morning. She took a bite of her lunch (spills be damned) and wheeled the office chair over to her laptop.

>So what happened Saturday? Alex was unusually cryptic when I talked to her this morning < sent 06/20/16 9:03 from 69.89.31.226 to 172.30.0.218

>It was a total bust, honestly. By the time I met up with Clark at some biotech company in Metropolis, the security there said it was a false alarm. < sent 06/20/16 9:05 from 172.30.0.218 to 69.89.31.226

>Strange< sent 06/20/16 9:06 from 69.89.31.226 to 172.30.0.218

>Tell me about it< sent 06/20/16 9:06 from 172.30.0.218 to 69.89.31.226

Lena shook her head, about to remove the messages from the Catco server, when her chewing slowed around a piece of chicken and lettuce. She reread the words in front of her twice, each time the text becoming increasingly blurry at the edges. She tried to scroll up, but her shaking hands fumbled with the scroll wheel on the mouse and she ended up going too far. She clenched her free hand against the side of the desk. She could feel the pulse in her thumb against the fibreboard underside.

Frantic, her eyes began to flitter around the room as her focus wavered, jumping from the sound of the reverberating AC to the pulsing light on Dilworth’s computer, asleep on the edge of the desk. It was suddenly so warm and claustrophobic in the office. It felt like the AC was just recirculating humid air. Thick. Heavy. Prickling on her skin.

Lena dropped her wrap onto its paper packaging. A chopped piece of pepper flew off, landing on the keyboard. Barely reaching for the trashcan in time, Lena leaned over in her chair and hurled.

Oh god. I’m going to be sick, she thought.

 Who was she kidding, she was already there.

 

Over the next half-hour, she tried to convince herself that she was just reading into things. That Kara’s exchange with Winn could mean nothing at all, but the evidence was there, and it was damning.

How could she even give the slightest iota of concern to her ridiculous Superfriends program now?

“Hi Shaun,” Lena spoke into the receiver, “I’m not feeling well and need to go home early. This is Lena by the way.”

She pulled her phone away from her ear after finishing the voicemail and blinked up at the harsh fluorescent lighting. The desk was a mess, but Dilworth’s opinion of her or her cleanliness was the last thing on her mind. Lena spared only a minute to clear away the unappetizing wrap and stack her DVDs in the corner by the CRT TV. She was out the front doors of Catco five minutes later.

Despite her growing headache and the panic that had now settled into a dull ache in her chest, Lena spent the early morning hours scouring her old AI project notes. She didn’t know what she was looking for in particular, only hoped she’d recognize it when she found it.

“You have reached Delta Point: your home for secure data centre solutions. We’re sorry we can’t come to the phone right now. Our hours are Monday through Friday, eight a.m. through—”

Lena hung up before the automated message could finish and looked over at the clock on the microwave above her oven. She could have sworn it was just 3:30; now the LED screen read 5:52. She’d reached a dead end, limited by the files she still had saved on her personal computer. It looked like she was going to have to wait if she wanted to access the back-up server her and Jack had leased during their start-up company days.

 

It felt like déjà vu when she awoke around noon. Her phone had slipped off the bed during the night and now sat lodged in the space between her bed frame and side table. There was a single new message notification lighting up the screen; Lena froze, thinking back to Kara. But this was from a number she hadn’t saved.

[(916) 866-1989] No problem! I hope you feel better. Don’t worry about helping set everything up for the new hire today. Let me know if you’re feeling better by Monday.

Right. Lena had forgotten she was supposed to meet her new assistant today.

She sent a curt reply to Shaun, before shrugging on her robe and pacing back into her kitchen. It was a mess. A stack of used dishes sat in her sink, unable to fit in the dishwasher. She had enough half-finished mugs strewn about her counter, it looked like she was recreating a scene from the movie Signs. Lena did her best to crowd the empty ones to the corner beside the soap dispenser, then dumped the rest, thankful she never took milk or cream in her coffee.

After filling her stomach with actual sustenance, Lena called the data centre and connected with a real person on the other end.

“Hi, yes, I was looking to access a server I was leasing from you,” she said, while pushing the crumbs from her toast around the plate with her coffee spoon.

The customer service agent asked for her information, her old address, and a PIN number she hadn’t the slightest clue about. She got lucky when she guessed Lilah’s birthday, remembering the Puppuccinos they’d pick up for her on May 28th every year.

“Miss Luthor?” the woman’s voice said a moment later.

“Yes?”

“I’m sorry, but your account with us was closed out a year ago. Was this a mistake?”

Lena bit the inside of her cheek, muttering “seriously Jack?” under her breath, before she cleared her throat and replied with an abrupt, “no.”

“Were you interested in re-opening an account?” the woman asked.

“Would you still have the data stored on the server available?”

It was a long shot, but Lena had to ask.

“Oh, of course not Ma’am; that would go against our privacy policy. I can direct you towards that and—”

“No, thank you,” Lena interrupted.

“Were you still interested in re-opening your account?”

“No.”

“So, what’s next?” Lena’s new assistant, Jess, asked.

She was hovering in the doorway to Dilworth’s office, Monday night. Lena huffed quietly, before pausing her movie and swivelling to face Jess. Lena could tell her assistant was trying very hard to remain impassive, but her eyes betrayed her, constantly glancing behind Lena at the TV that was currently paused on a shot of Joyce Hyser striding through a parking lot in a men’s jacket and dark shades.

“You finished setting up your desk phone?” Lena asked, leg bouncing impatiently.

“Uh, yeah,” Jess said.

“Huh.”

Lena pinched her lips into a fine line. Why had they insisted on her hiring an assistant again? Lena’s indecision was obviously not enough of a deterrent for Jess, who continued to lean against the doorframe.

“Do you like 80s teen comedies?” Lena spoke up a second later.

It was definitely not what Jess was expecting; she opened her mouth, then closed it, before deciding on a nod.

Without saying another word, Lena leaned sideways and dragged an empty office chair, probably older than the movie on screen, adjacent to her own. She patted the faded blue canvas on the seat and motioned for Jess to join her.

They spent the next forty-five minutes there together, even sharing a bag of almonds Lena had packed as a snack, until a pop-up on Lena’s computer forced her to hit pause.

“I need to—” She pointed at her computer and Jess nodded, taking her leave.

There was an error notification on her computer; the Superfriends program had run through every available message and without further instructions, had stalled. Lena closed the pop-up and opened a separate window to compare the accuracy of the program with her own sifting these past couple months. It had done better than she’d expected, catching 99.3% of all the messages she’d found and even identifying thirty-eight other potential sensitive emails she hadn’t seen before.

Lena only wished she’d achieved the same level of success this past weekend as she did now. Unfortunately, it was more than impossible to diagnose weak points in her old AI program when she was missing at least half of it and operating off of code that for all she knew, had been completely overhauled since she’d last played around with it on her virtual testing environment.

It wasn’t quite time for her lunch, but Lena didn’t think she could stand to sit in the stifling office any longer. She’d forgotten to prepare anything for her lunches this week and found herself retracing her steps back to the only place in the city that didn’t seem to send her into a spiral of self-pity and frustration these days.

“Welcome to Wrap Zone,” a guy’s voice called across the liminal space between the buzzing downtown streets of National City and the empty dining area of the small restaurant.

Lena glanced up, barely acknowledging the teenager, before shuffling over to the start of the order counter. She began to rattle off her usual order. There was late 2000s music playing quietly over the speakers that someone in the kitchen was humming along to.

“Would you like to make that a combo?” the guy asked, breaking Lena from her daze.

“No thank you,” she replied and reached into her purse to feel around for her wallet.

“What about a bag?” he followed up with, even though he’d already slid the wrap across the counter.

Lena drew one brow up and shook her head. “I don’t think so,” she muttered, taking her food.

When her eyes glanced back up at the guy, his attention had shifted behind her. A faint electronic ding came from the front of the restaurant as the door closed behind a new customer.

“Welcome to Wrap Zone,” the guy drawled, again.

He was already moving back to the other end of the counter, but the new customer hadn’t stepped away from the door.

Just as Lena went to turn and tuck her wallet back into her purse, a familiar voice sounded behind her.

“Lena?”

Notes for the Chapter:

Next up: Kara

How many 80s movies references can I throw in, you might ask? Many more.

16. Kara

Summary for the Chapter:

Kara finally finds Lena, but it's not what she's expecting.

Notes for the Chapter:

Short Kara Chapter, before VERY long Lena one next.

Also Hello, I thought I would actually update this again.
Epilogue added to final chapter count.

“Lena?”

Kara scanned the lobby, hearing only the faint buzz of servers in the distance. There were no signs that indicated security had given her the correct floor for IT; however, the placards outside the offices said things like Cybersecurity Services and Technician 2, so she figured she was in the right place.

The floor was just as quiet as it had been the previous Friday when Kara had stopped by, looking for Lena. She had thought about taking the elevator up to the IT floor on more than one occasion, but actually stepping inside and hitting a different number button than she did every other morning was a lot. The 1st floor lobby of Catco had been eerily quiet except for a single security guard behind the front desk. She’d needed to scan her employee badge to get inside the building so late at night, and then once more in the elevator to access the upper floors.

Kara pushed past the clear glass doors then and moved down the hall, keeping an eye out for Lena’s name. She made it all the way around the small maze of offices but still, there was no sign of Lena. She tried to check the mailroom even, but the door was locked. Kara suspected breaking into it wouldn’t be worth the trouble.

Does Lena even work here still? Kara wondered.

She’d woken one morning a couple weeks ago to an email from Wrapzone stating her scheduled delivery for Lena had been unsuccessful and that while they couldn’t refund her for the purchase, they would offer her 20% off one adult entrée during her next visit.

Kara had considered reaching out so many times, but just thinking back to the messages she’d last sent Lena made her stomach tumble in very unpleasant ways. She never got hungover or sick, but if this was what nausea felt like, Kara was eternally grateful for her body’s resilience on Earth.

There was also the issue that there was no way she’d be able to fit everything she needed to say to Lena in one text message. This brought her to calling, which had seemed like a decent idea for about twenty seconds, before Kara realized that option two had even more ways of going wrong. What if she answered, but wouldn’t hear Kara out? What if she’d already been blocked? What if Lena ignored the call? Kara couldn’t leave a voicemail! She didn’t even know the proper etiquette around voicemails.

This was why Kara had decided a few days ago that in-person was the only safe option—actually finding her though, was slowly becoming a bigger issue.

Kara retraced her steps past the empty offices until she made it back to the set of glass doors. Just as she was reaching for the handle, one of the elevators on the other side of the lobby dinged and the light above the right one lit up.

Someone else was there.

Crap.

Kara scrambled with the door and nearly faced planted into the transparent surface. She was able to right herself in time to see a woman stepping off, head drawn down to the phone in her hands. Was this Lena?

Kara paused her movements, not wanting to startle the woman. Thankfully, she looked up after a second and noticed Kara lingering by the doors.

“Hello,” the woman said, scrutinizing Kara closely.

She tucked some of her glossy, black hair behind her ear and pocketed her phone in her cardigan pocket.

“Hi,” Kara replied, just as hesitantly. “I was looking for Lena.”

The woman’s confusion dissipated, and a small note of interest overtook her features.

“Lena?” she asked, imploringly.

Had Lena quit after all? Was this her replacement?

“Lena isn’t here right now,” the woman continued, cutting off Kara’s internal panic. “I’m her assistant though. Maybe I can help. What is it you need Lena for?”

“Uh,” Kara laughed nervously to herself, adjusting the long swoop of hair that she’d accidentally left out of her braid earlier. She’d arrived directly after Supergirl duties and hadn’t thought to re-do her hair, knowing Lena was already aware of her secret identity anyway. “I don’t think you can help. Do you know when she’ll be back?”

The woman set her bag down on the table in the middle of the lobby and rooted around her bag until she found a shaker bottle. She uncapped it and took a swig, before glancing back up at Kara again.

“I’m not sure,” she finally responded. “I just started, but Miss Luthor is usually back from lunch by 1 a.m.”

“Oh. Okay. That’s good. Thanks.”

The woman forced a smile, before pulling her cell phone back out. Kara nodded once, before heading for the elevators.

 

There were far less people at Wrapzone in the middle of the night than the couple of times Kara had gone by during the day. Tonight, there was a sole man working behind the counter and a customer heading out as Kara opened the front door.

“Welcome to Wrapzone,” the man drawled, not even looking up from the register.

Kara approached the counter and waited until he finally made eye contact with her. When he did, he didn’t look impressed. His eyed flicked up once more to her business casual chinos and tucked in button-up, then down the counter to where people usually formed a line.

“I was wondering if you’ve seen a woman in here tonight,” Kara said, before he could open his mouth to make some snide comment.

The change in script threw him off for a second, before his eyelids drooped and he rolled his eyes.

“Any women or one in particular?” he asked, failing to conceal an impish sneer as he looked down to close the register.

“One particular woman, yes,” Kara clarified.

“And what does this one particular woman look like?”

“She’s… Her name’s Lena.”

“Okay…”

“She’s a little quirky and awkward sometimes but hides it very well.”

The man blinked back at her like she was insane. He sort of had point, Kara admitted, but she’d never actually met Lena is person, so what else was she to go off of?

Quickly, she drew her phone out of her pocket, intending to Google Lena’s name, when another Wrapzone employee stepped through the swinging door to the back.

“You’re looking for Lena?” the girl—Margaret, her name tag read—said.

“Yes!” Kara confirmed, maybe a little too loud. “Has she been by tonight?”

Margaret pulled off a set of clear plastic gloves and dropped them in a bin, before approaching the counter where the dressings sat in their labeled tubes.

“You Kara?” Margaret asked instead.

Her brows rose in interest when Kara didn’t immediately deny the assumption.

“Yeah, she was here tonight,” Margaret continued, before Kara could say a thing. “She was in here maybe twenty minutes ago.”

“Oh.” Kara looked around the small shop, then out past the brightly lit windows to the dark street beyond.

As if reading her mind, Margaret continued, “She ordered her usual then left with some dude. They went that way,” she said pointing North toward the University.”

Kara mulled over her words, before she belatedly recalled one specific detail.  

“Was he British?”

 

There was no telling where Lena and her ex had gone, but if Kara was lucky, they’d still be out. A quick search on Kara’s phone told her there were only a few places open this late at night. Under the cover of darkness, she made quick work checking every location North of the Wrapzone.

After finding the first two restaurants closed despite their 24-hour claim on Google, Kara landed in an alley next to National City University’s downtown campus where her next three locations were.

She stepped out of the alley, forgoing her braid for a simple ponytail this time, and tucked her shirt back into her trousers. A cluster of brick buildings encircled a large courtyard where flags sporting National City University colours hung from lampposts.

A late-night coffee shop had its lights on, on the other side of the courtyard, so Kara went around the perimeter until she was just a block away. Inside, sitting at a table by the window, Kara immediately spotted the ex with his neatly trimmed beard, dark skin, and form-fitting sweater.

Another swell of nausea rose up, pressing uncomfortably in her chest, before she forced her gaze away from him to the woman sitting across the table. She had a mug raised to her lips. When she lowered it to the table, she smiled and instantly, Kara felt her world drop away from her. She laid a hand against the building next to her and forced her eyes away before either of them spotted her.

Holy actual Fuck.

What was she doing here?

Kara struggled for a moment as images of the striking woman from work and that bar merged with everything she knew about Lena. Lena from IT and the striking woman. How had she not realized they were one in the same?

Without looking back their direction, Kara fled into the alley and took off. She had no destination in mind as the cool air rushed past her skin, becoming heavier with moisture as she flew through the dense evening clouds. Finally, she slowed down, somewhere over the ocean just West of the city.

Despite how foolish and unreasonable the emotion was, the sudden and red-hot frustration she’d felt when she’d first learned that Lena had been reading their messages, flared up again. It just wasn’t fair to feel so in the dark. Lena knew so much about who Kara was and up until a few minutes ago, Kara hadn’t even realized that the two women she’d developed embarrassing crushes over were the same person.  

Rao, what an idiot she must look like. Had that been what Lena and her ex were talking about in the coffee shop?

‘Does Supergirl really think she’s being clever with that disguise?’ she imagined Lena saying.

But no. That wasn’t right. There was no reason to believe Lena would betray her like that, even if Kara had never really known her. She’d even promised—after Kara had blown up her phone one emotionally fueled afternoon—that her secret was always safe with her.

The one person that was the most concerned with Kara’s secret identity had taken better than Kara herself. Just four hours after Kara’s serious onslaught of texts to Lena, she’d shown up at Alex’s apartment with an apology six-pack of beer and pizza.

Her sister had been reasonably suspicious at first notice of Kara’s offerings and invited her in, moving directly to the couch where all proper breakdowns occurred. But after coming clean about the whole thing, Alex hadn’t quite reacted how Kara thought she would.

“She’s basically your nerdy knight in tweed pant suits,” Alex had remarked. “I like her.”

“What?” Kara replied, surprised. “You like her?”

“Well yeah,” Alex replied, taking a sip of beer. “She’s saved your ass countless times by now and put Winn in his place. My hero.”

Kara scowled. “You don’t even know if she wears tweed pant suits.”

Alex shrugged. “Well, what does she wear?”

“I don’t know,” Kara replied, hiding her face behind a slice. “I haven’t met her before.”

“Hmm,” Alex said, thoughtfully.

“What?”

“Nothing, nothing. I just—I’ve never been so smitten over someone I’ve never even met.”

Alex, of course, had many, many follow-up questions after that. Thankfully, her apprehension eventually lessened to such a degree that she suggested maybe it was Lena who was owed the pizza and drinks.

“How bad is it?” Kara had asked, after showing Alex the text messages.

Her sister’s delayed response spoke volumes on its own.

“I’ll fix this,” Kara admitted by the end of the night.

How, she still wasn’t sure yet.

 

It was almost 1:30 in the morning by the time Kara had calmed down enough to safely fly back to the city and navigate her way to Alex’s building. Her sister was waiting with the window open by the time she arrived.

“I’m sorry to wake you,” she said, sheepishly.

Alex shrugged. “I was still awake, if you can believe that.”
